### Questions

1. **What does Yuki decide to do?**  
   yuki-san wa nani o kimemashita ka? (ユキさんは何を決めましたか？)
   - A. Go up in winter to help Kenji when the snow piles up.
   - B. Visit Kenji when the cherry blossoms come out up there, about a month after they come out at home.
   - C. Try to talk Kenji into a university nearer home, since he's no good in the cold either.

2. **It's early April. Kenji has moved to Hokkaido, where the cherry blossoms won't be out for weeks. He calls and asks, "sotchi, sakura dou?" (how are the blossoms there?). The ones near you are out. Tell him, using saku.** *(say it)*  
   sotchi, sakura dou? (そっち、桜どう？)

3. **It's a scorching day in August. Yuki suggests eating lunch outside: "ohiru, soto de tabenai?" Say no: you just can't take the heat. Use atsusa ni yowai.** *(say it)*  
   ohiru, soto de tabenai? (お昼、外で食べない？)

4. **Kenji complains about summers at home. What did he say about the cicadas?**  
   kenji wa semi ni tsuite nan to iimashita ka? (ケンジはセミについて何と言いましたか？)
   - A. *asa kara semi ga utatte, nerarenai shi* (the cicadas sing from first thing, so I can't sleep)
   - B. *asa kara semi ga natte, nerarenai shi* (the cicadas ring from first thing, so I can't sleep)
   - C. *asa kara semi ga naite, nerarenai shi* (the cicadas call from first thing, so I can't sleep)

5. **Yuki warns Kenji about winter in Hokkaido. What does she say about the snow?**  
   hokkaidou no fuyu ni tsuite, yuki-san wa nan to iimashita ka? (北海道の冬について、ユキさんは何と言いましたか？)
   - A. A lot of it builds up, and once it's lying on the ground it's hard work.
   - B. It hardly ever falls there, but when it does, it's freezing.
   - C. It falls almost every day, but it's always gone by the next morning.

### New words

- **hokkaidou** (北海道): Hokkaido, Japan's snowy northern island
- **natsu** (夏): summer
- **fuyu** (冬): winter
- **sakura** (桜): cherry blossoms
- **furu** (降る): fall (rain, snow)
- **samusa** (寒さ): the cold (samui + -sa, like atsusa)

<details>
<summary>Answers and transcript</summary>

1. **B**. Lines 1-2: Kenji is going to university in Hokkaido, partly because he can't take the heat. Lines 13-15: the cherry blossoms come out about a month later up there, so Yuki says she'll visit when they do (atchi de sakura ga saitara, asobi ni iku) and get to see them twice this year. A is wrong: Kenji asks her to come and help when the snow piles up (line 16), but she says no, because she can't take the cold herself (line 17). C is wrong: Yuki teases him about the cold (line 11), but she never tries to change his mind.
2. Say they're out with saiteru: mou saiteru yo (they're already out). Saita says they've come out, saiteru says they're out now, and mada saitenai is not out yet. Don't reach for deru: hana ga deru isn't said. For example: *mou saiteru yo. ima ga ichiban kirei.* (They're already out. They're at their best right now.); *kinou saita yo. sotchi wa mada saitenai desho?* (They came out yesterday. Yours aren't out yet, are they?). Yuki and Kenji use saku for the blossoms in lines 13 to 15: atchi tte sakura ga saku no, osoi n da yo ne? (they come out late up there, don't they?), osoku saku tte (they come out later), and atchi de sakura ga saitara (when they come out up there). Flowers coming out is saku, and flowers that are out now are saiteru. English 'the blossoms are out' tempts deru, but hana ga deru isn't said, and hiraku (open) is for describing petals, not for chat. When the petals come down, it's chiru: mou chitchatta. In casual talk ga usually drops: sakura saita?
3. Explain with atsusa ni yowai, often as atsusa ni yowai n da (the thing is, I'm no good in the heat). Keep the ni: atsusa ga yowai would mean the heat itself is weak. For example: *gomen, atsusa ni yowai n da. naka de tabenai?* (Sorry, I can't take the heat. Can we eat inside?); *kyou wa chotto. atsusa ni yowai kara, naka ni shiyou yo.* (Not today. I'm no good in the heat, so let's eat inside.). Kenji's reason for Hokkaido is atsusa ni yowai kara (line 2), Yuki agrees he always has been (line 3), and he sets it against the cold in line 10: atsusa ni wa yowai kedo, samusa ni wa yowakunai. Yuki turns it round on him at the end: samusa ni yowai kara (line 17). Yowai with ni means you can't handle something, like osake ni yowai (can't hold your drink), and with atsusa the ni stays, never ga. Atsusa and samusa are atsui and samui made into nouns with -sa. Atsui no nigate (I can't do heat) is just as everyday; the opposite is atsusa ni tsuyoi.
4. **C**. Line 4: asa kara semi ga naite, nerarenai shi. Insects, birds and animals all naku (鳴く), which sounds the same as naku 'cry' (泣く): semi ga naku, tori ga naku, neko ga naku. A carries the English verb over: utau is singing a song, and tori ga utau is poetry, not chat. B uses naru, which is for things that make a sound, like denwa ga naru or kaminari ga naru: animals naku, things naru. Yuki says it too (line 5: semi ga naku to, natsu tte kanji), and Kenji again in line 6 (semi anmari nakanai).
5. **A**. Line 7: atchi, yuki sugoku tsumoru yo (the snow really piles up there). Line 9: futteru dake nara kirei da kedo, tsumoru to taihen da yo (pretty while it's coming down, hard work once it settles). Furu is snow coming down; tsumoru is snow building up and staying, so yuki tsumotteru means there's snow on the ground. B is wrong: she says a lot of it piles up. C is wrong: snow that tsumoru stays put; snow melting is tokeru. Snow stuck on things, like a car, is tsuku: kuruma ni yuki ga tsuiteru.

|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Yuki | kenji, hokkaidou no daigaku ni hairu n da tte? nande hokkaidou ni shita no? | Kenji, I hear you're going to university in Hokkaido? Why Hokkaido? |
| 2 | Kenji | atsusa ni yowai kara. kotchi no natsu, mou muri. | 'Cause I can't take the heat. I'm done with summers here. |
| 3 | Yuki | e, sore dake? maa, kenji wa mukashi kara atsusa ni yowai kedo. | What, that's it? I mean, you've always been hopeless in the heat, but still. |

| 14 | Kenji | un. netto de shirabetara, kotchi yori ikkagetsu gurai osoku saku tte. | Yeah. I looked it up online: they come out about a month later than here. |
| 15 | Yuki | jaa, atchi de sakura ga saitara, asobi ni iku! kotoshi wa nikai mireru ne. | Then when they come out up there, I'm coming to visit! I'll get to see them twice this year. |
| 16 | Kenji | e, kuru no? jaa, yuki ga tsumottara, tetsudai ni kite yo. | Wait, you're coming? Then come and help me out when the snow piles up. |

## 87. A dog bed for the cat

*Your place · Polite dialogue · 17 lines · Satou-san, Yuki*

Saturday morning at the door of Yuki's small rented flat. Satou-san, the elderly neighbour, turns up with a big parcel of hers that the driver took to his flat by mistake last night.

**Collocations:** *heya o kariru* (rent a room or flat); *semai heya* (a small, cramped room); *inu o kau* (have / keep a dog); *nimotsu ga todoku* (a package arrives, gets delivered).

**Review:** *netto de shiraberu* (look something up online); *yousu o miru* (wait and see; check on (someone)).

### Audio script

| Speaker | Voice |
|---|---|
| Satou-san | male, 70s, polite, kind, unhurried |
| Yuki | female, late 20s, bright and friendly |

| # | Speaker | Japanese | Kana (if the voice misreads a kanji) |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Satou-san | ユキさん、おはようございます。昨日の夜、ユキさんの荷物がうちに届いたんですよ。部屋を間違えたみたいで。 | ゆきさん、おはようございます。きのうのよる、ゆきさんのにもつがうちにとどいたんですよ。へやをまちがえたみたいで。 |

| 6 | Yuki | はい。この部屋を借りるときに、小さい犬ならいいって言われました。 | はい。このへやをかりるときに、ちいさいいぬならいいっていわれました。 |
| 7 | Satou-san | それはよかったですね。私も昔、狭い部屋を借りて、犬を飼っていましたよ。 | それはよかったですね。わたしもむかし、せまいへやをかりて、いぬをかっていましたよ。 |

### Questions

1. **What happens in this conversation?**  
   nani ga arimashita ka? (何がありましたか？)
   - A. The dog bed Yuki ordered for the small dog she's getting turns out to be for a big dog, so it goes to Satou-san's cat, and she'll buy a small one once the dog has come.
   - B. Yuki finds out dogs aren't allowed in her rented flat, so Satou-san offers to keep the dog at his place.
   - C. Satou-san ordered a big dog bed by mistake, so he gives it to Yuki for the small dog she's getting next week.

2. **Satou-san asks if Yuki's allowed to keep a dog where she lives. What does she say?**  
   yuki-san wa nan to iimashita ka? (ユキさんは何と言いましたか？)
   - A. *kono heya o kasu toki ni, chiisai inu nara ii tte iwaremashita.* (When I let this flat out, they told me a small dog would be fine.)
   - B. *kono heya o rentaru suru toki ni, chiisai inu nara ii tte iwaremashita.* (When I hired this flat, they told me a small dog would be fine.)
   - C. *kono heya o kariru toki ni, chiisai inu nara ii tte iwaremashita.* (When I rented this flat, they told me a small dog would be fine.)

3. **You've just moved into a new flat, and Kenta has come round for the first time. At the door he says, "hee, koko ga atarashii heya? haitte ii?" (Oh, so this is the new place? Can I come in?) Invite him in the modest way a host does, saying it's a small place.** *(say it)*  
   hee, koko ga atarashii heya? haitte ii? (へえ、ここが新しい部屋？入っていい？)

4. **Kenta shows you photos of a friend's new puppy and says, "kawaii desho? inu, suki?" (Cute, right? Do you like dogs?) You love dogs, and your family has one at home. Tell him.** *(say it)*  
   kawaii desho? inu, suki? (かわいいでしょ？犬、好き？)

5. **Satou-san brings Yuki her parcel. What had happened to it?**  
   yuki-san no nimotsu wa, dou narimashita ka? (ユキさんの荷物は、どうなりましたか？)
   - A. Yuki was out when it came last night, so the driver left it with Satou-san next door.
   - B. The driver took it to Satou-san's flat by mistake last night, so it never reached Yuki, though she'd waited in all day.
   - C. It was left outside Yuki's door last night, and Satou-san took it in so it wouldn't go missing.

### New words

- **beddo** (ベッド): bed (inu no beddo = a dog bed)
- **neko** (猫): cat (uchi no neko = my cat; Satou-san's is called Tama)
- **hiroi** (広い): roomy, spacious (the opposite of semai)

<details>
<summary>Answers and transcript</summary>

1. **A**. Lines 1-4: Satou-san brings round Yuki's parcel, which went to his flat by mistake. It's a dog bed, because she's getting a dog next week (raishuu kara, inu o kau n desu). Lines 11-12: when she takes it out, it's a bed for a big dog, and she realises she ordered the wrong one (machigaete tanonjatta). Line 14: a big dog won't work in her cramped flat. Lines 15-16: Satou-san asks for the bed for his cat, Tama, and Yuki will buy a small one after the dog comes and she's seen how it settles in (yousu o mite). B is wrong: when Yuki rented the flat she was told a small dog would be fine (line 6), and Satou-san never offers to keep the dog. C swaps them round: it's Yuki's parcel (yuki-san no nimotsu, line 1), and the bed goes from Yuki to Satou-san's cat, not the other way.
2. **C**. Line 6: kono heya o kariru toki ni, chiisai inu nara ii tte iwaremashita. Japanese has no separate verb for renting a place: the tenant kariru (borrows), the same verb as borrowing a pen. Satou-san uses it for the poky room he had years ago (semai heya o karite, line 7), and Yuki says a big dog would mean renting somewhere roomier (motto hiroi heya o karinai to, line 14). A is wrong: kasu (lend) is the landlord's side, so heya o kasu toki would mean when she let the flat out to someone else. B is wrong: rentaru suru is for hiring things like a car or a kimono for a day, not a flat you live in.
3. Use the host's set line with semai heya: semai heya da kedo, douzo (it's a tiny place, but come in). For example: *un, semai heya da kedo, douzo.* (Sure. It's a tiny place, but come on in.); *semai heya desu kedo, douzo haitte kudasai.* (It's only a small place, but please, come in.). Yuki says just this to Satou-san in line 10: semai heya desu kedo, douzo. It's the host's modest welcome, and a guest will often answer hiroi ne! (it's roomy!). A cramped room or flat is semai, the opposite of hiroi, as in line 14 (kono semai heya de desu ka? ... motto hiroi heya). Chiisai heya is fine for a room's size (chiisai heya ga futatsu aru), but for the cramped feeling, and in this welcome, people say semai. Kenta's a friend, so da kedo. The second model is the polite version, for someone like Tanaka-san. Semai tokoro da kedo, douzo is a common variant.
4. Say you love them, then say your family keeps one with inu o kau in the -teru form: uchi mo inu katteru n da. For example: *un, daisuki! uchi mo inu katteru n da.* (Yeah, I love them! We've got a dog at home too.); *hai, daisuki desu. uchi de mo inu o katte iru n desu.* (Yes, I love them. We have a dog at home too.). Keeping a pet is kau, which sounds just like kau 'buy'. Yuki uses it for the dog she's getting (raishuu kara, inu o kau n desu, line 4), and Satou-san for the dog he had years ago (inu o katte imashita, line 7). His nanika katta n desu ka? in line 3 is the other kau, 'buy', and the context keeps them apart. Having a pet is almost always in the -teru form: inu katteru. Inu o motte iru sounds like you own a dog the way you own a bag, and inu ga iru (there's a dog) also works. The second model is the polite version, for someone like Satou-san.
5. **B**. Line 1: kinou no yoru, yuki-san no nimotsu ga uchi ni todoita n desu yo. heya o machigaeta mitai de. Todoku means 'reach', and here the parcel reached the wrong flat. Line 2: Yuki waited in all day, but nimotsu ga todokanakute (it never reached her). Things sent to you todoku, the same verb as for letters and messages. Tsuku is for people and trains getting somewhere (eki ni tsuku), so nimotsu ga tsuita is less usual. A is wrong: Yuki was in, waiting all day (zutto matteta), and the driver got the flat wrong. C is wrong: it never got to Yuki's door. It was delivered straight to Satou-san's place (uchi ni todoita).

| # | Speaker | Romaji | English |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Satou-san | yuki-san, ohayou gozaimasu. kinou no yoru, yuki-san no nimotsu ga uchi ni todoita n desu yo. heya o machigaeta mitai de. | Good morning, Yuki-san. Your parcel came to my place last night. They must have got the wrong flat. |
| 2 | Yuki | a, yokatta! kinou zutto matteta noni, nimotsu ga todokanakute. arigatou gozaimasu. | Oh, thank goodness! I waited in all day yesterday, and it never came. Thank you so much. |
| 3 | Satou-san | kekkou ookii desu ne. nanika katta n desu ka? | It's quite big, isn't it? Did you buy something? |
| 4 | Yuki | jitsu wa, inu no beddo na n desu. raishuu kara, inu o kau n desu. | It's a dog bed, actually. I'm getting a dog next week. |
| 5 | Satou-san | hee, inu desu ka. demo, koko de inu o katte mo ii n desu ka? | Oh, a dog! But are you allowed to keep a dog here? |
| 6 | Yuki | hai. kono heya o kariru toki ni, chiisai inu nara ii tte iwaremashita. | Yes. When I rented this place, they told me a small dog would be fine. |
| 7 | Satou-san | sore wa yokatta desu ne. watashi mo mukashi, semai heya o karite, inu o katte imashita yo. | That's good. Years ago I rented a poky little room too, and kept a dog in it. |
| 8 | Yuki | uchi mo semai heya na node, chiisai inu ni shita n desu. beddo mo, netto de chanto shirabete kara kaimashita. | My place is tiny too, so I went for a small dog. And I looked the bed up properly online before I bought it. |
| 9 | Satou-san | sasuga desu ne. donna beddo ka, mite mitai desu ne. | How thorough of you. I'd love to see what it's like. |
| 10 | Yuki | jaa, ima dashite mimasu ne. semai heya desu kedo, douzo. | Then I'll take it out now. It's a tiny place, but come on in. |
| 11 | Satou-san | are? yuki-san, sono beddo, sugoku ookii desu ne. | Oh? Yuki-san, that bed's awfully big. |
| 12 | Yuki | e? uso! kore, ookii inu no beddo desu. machigaete tanonjatta mitai desu. | What? No way! This is a bed for a big dog. I must have ordered the wrong one. |
| 13 | Satou-san | jaa, ookii inu o kattara dou desu ka? | Well then, why not get a big dog? |
| 14 | Yuki | kono semai heya de desu ka? muri desu yo. ookii inu nara, motto hiroi heya o karinai to. | In this cramped little place? No way. A big dog would mean renting somewhere roomier. |
| 15 | Satou-san | sore nara, sono beddo, uchi no neko ni moratte mo ii desu ka? tama wa, ookii beddo ga daisuki na n desu. | In that case, could I have that bed for my cat? Tama loves a big bed. |
| 16 | Yuki | tama-chan ni desu ka? mochiron desu. jaa, chiisai beddo wa, inu ga kite kara, yousu o mite kaimasu ne. | For Tama? Of course. Then I'll buy a small bed once the dog's here and I've seen how it settles in. |
| 17 | Satou-san | sore ga ii desu ne. tsugi no nimotsu ga mata uchi ni todoitara, motte kimasu yo. | Good idea. And if your next parcel turns up at my place again, I'll bring it round. |

</details>

## 88. The same worry

*Worries and advice · Casual dialogue · 18 lines · Sora, Mai*

A quiet weekday evening at the café where Sora works part-time. Mai, a regular, has been sitting alone over her coffee looking down, so Sora comes over to her table.

**Collocations:** *nayami ga aru* (have something on your mind); *soudan ga aru* (want to ask your advice); *soudan ni noru* (give advice, hear someone out); *honne o iu* (say what you really think).

**Review:** *keikaku o tateru* (make a plan); *chuushi ni naru* (be called off).

### Audio script

| Speaker | Voice |
|---|---|
| Sora | male, mid 20s, light voice, playful |
| Mai | female, late 20s, warm, a little shy |

| # | Speaker | Japanese | Kana (if the voice misreads a kanji) |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Sora | マイさん、元気ないね。なんか悩みあるの？ | まいさん、げんきないね。なんかなやみあるの？ |
| 2 | Mai | うん。ソラくん、今いい？ちょっと相談があるんだけど。 | うん。そらくん、いまいい？ちょっとそうだんがあるんだけど。 |
| 3 | Sora | いいよ、今ひまだし。僕でよかったら、相談乗るよ。 | いいよ、いまひまだし。ぼくでよかったら、そうだんのるよ。 |
| 4 | Mai | ありがとう。今度の週末、ユキとキャンプに行くの。 | ありがとう。こんどのしゅうまつ、ゆきときゃんぷにいくの。 |
| 5 | Sora | へえ、いいね。でも、楽しみじゃないの？本音言っていいよ。 | へえ、いいね。でも、たのしみじゃないの？ほんねいっていいよ。 |
| 6 | Mai | 本音を言うと、あんまり行きたくないんだ。外で寝るの、苦手で。 | ほんねをいうと、あんまりいきたくないんだ。そとでねるの、にがてで。 |
| 7 | Sora | そっか。それ、ユキさんに言った？ | そっか。それ、ゆきさんにいった？ |
| 8 | Mai | 言えないよ。私が前に、キャンプ楽しそうって言ったから、ユキが計画立ててくれたの。 | いえないよ。わたしがまえに、きゃんぷたのしそうっていったから、ゆきがけいかくたててくれたの。 |
| 9 | Sora | でも、言わないと、週末ずっと外で寝ることになるよ。本音、言ってみたら？ | でも、いわないと、しゅうまつずっとそとでねることになるよ。ほんね、いってみたら？ |
| 10 | Mai | そうだよね。あ、ユキから連絡来た。 | そうだよね。あ、ゆきかられんらくきた。 |
| 11 | Sora | なんて？ | なんて？ |
| 12 | Mai | 「ちょっと相談があるんだけど。本音を言うと、キャンプ苦手なんだ」って。 | 「ちょっとそうだんがあるんだけど。ほんねをいうと、きゃんぷにがてなんだ」って。 |
| 13 | Sora | え、ユキさんも同じ悩みがあったんだ！ | え、ゆきさんもおなじなやみがあったんだ！ |
| 14 | Mai | うん。私が行きたそうだったから、計画立てたんだって。 | うん。わたしがいきたそうだったから、けいかくたてたんだって。 |
| 15 | Sora | なんだ。じゃあ、マイさんもユキさんも、行きたくなかったんだ。 | なんだ。じゃあ、まいさんもゆきさんも、いきたくなかったんだ。 |
| 16 | Mai | そうみたい。キャンプは中止になりそうだけど、かわりにユキとここに来るね。 | そうみたい。きゃんぷはちゅうしになりそうだけど、かわりにゆきとここにくるね。 |
| 17 | Sora | いいね、待ってる。また悩みがあったら、いつでも相談に乗るよ。 | いいね、まってる。またなやみがあったら、いつでもそうだんにのるよ。 |
| 18 | Mai | ありがとう。ソラくんに相談に乗ってもらって、よかった。 | ありがとう。そらくんにそうだんにのってもらって、よかった。 |

### Questions

1. **What happened with Mai's worry?**  
   mai-san no nayami wa, dou narimashita ka? (マイさんの悩みは、どうなりましたか？)
   - A. Mai was afraid the camping trip would be called off, but Yuki's message says it's still on.
   - B. Mai didn't want to go camping but couldn't tell Yuki, then found out Yuki didn't want to go either.
   - C. Mai was looking forward to camping, but Yuki admitted she doesn't like it, so now Mai is disappointed.

2. **After hearing Yuki's message, Sora says, "yuki-san mo onaji nayami ga atta n da!" What does he mean?**  
   sora-kun wa, nani ga wakarimashita ka? (ソラくんは、何がわかりましたか？)
   - A. Yuki had made other plans for the same weekend.
   - B. Yuki wants to ask Sora's advice too.
   - C. Yuki had been quietly worrying about the same thing as Mai.

3. **How does Mai bring up her problem with Sora?**  
   mai-san wa, sora-kun ni nan to iimashita ka? (マイさんは、ソラくんに何と言いましたか？)
   - A. *chotto soudan ga aru n da kedo* (there's something I'd like your advice on)
   - B. *soudan ni noru yo* (I'm happy to hear you out)
   - C. *chotto adobaisu kudasai* (give me some advice, please)

4. **Over dinner, your friend Kenta sighs and says, "saikin, shigoto de iroiro atte sa. chotto tsukarechatta." Offer to hear him out, using soudan ni noru.** *(say it)*  
   saikin, shigoto de iroiro atte sa. chotto tsukarechatta. (最近、仕事でいろいろあってさ。ちょっと疲れちゃった。)

5. **Yuki shows you a hat she's just bought: "atarashii boushi katta n da. dou? kawaii desho?" Honestly, you think it looks a bit odd. Tell her what you really think, using honne o iu.** *(say it)*  
   atarashii boushi katta n da. dou? kawaii desho? (新しい帽子買ったんだ。どう？かわいいでしょ？)

### New words

- **kyanpu** (キャンプ): camping, a camping trip (kyanpu ni iku: go camping)

<details>
<summary>Answers and transcript</summary>

1. **B**. Line 6: Mai admits she doesn't really want to go (honne o iu to, anmari ikitakunai n da). Line 8: she can't tell Yuki, who planned the trip because Mai once said camping sounded fun. Lines 12-14: Yuki's message says she doesn't like camping either and only planned it because Mai seemed keen. Line 16: the trip will probably be called off (chuushi ni narisou), and they'll come to the café instead. A is wrong: Mai was dreading the trip, not afraid of losing it, and the message never says it's still on. C is wrong: Mai wasn't looking forward to it (line 6), and she ends up relieved, thanking Sora (line 18), not disappointed.
2. **C**. Line 13: nayami ga aru means having something on your mind, a problem you keep turning over, and onaji nayami is the same worry. Yuki didn't want to go camping either (line 12: honne o iu to, kyanpu nigate na n da) but kept it to herself because she thought Mai wanted to go (line 14). Sora uses the same phrase to ask what's wrong in line 1 (nanka nayami aru no?) and to offer help in line 17 (mata nayami ga attara). A mixes it up with having plans (yotei ga aru); nobody mentions other plans. B mixes it up with soudan ga aru: Yuki's message opens with chotto soudan ga aru n da kedo (line 12), but she's asking Mai, not Sora.
3. **A**. Line 2: Mai opens with "chotto soudan ga aru n da kedo", the everyday way to run something by a friend. The one with the problem has a soudan, and the sentence trails off on kedo so the friend can say go ahead. Yuki's message opens the same way in line 12. B is the friend's side: Sora answers with soudan noru yo in line 3, so from Mai it would mean she's offering to hear him out. C is 'give me advice' word for word. adobaisu kudasai sounds abrupt between friends, and asking someone to help you think something through is soudan, not adobaisu.
4. Offer to listen with soudan ni noru, the way Sora does: watashi (or boku) de yokattara, soudan noru yo (if I can help, I'm all ears), or itsudemo soudan ni noru yo (I'm always here to talk). For example: *sou na n da. watashi de yokattara, soudan noru yo.* (Oh, really? If I can help, I'm all ears.); *taihen da ne. itsudemo soudan ni noru kara, nandemo itte ne.* (That sounds rough. I'm always here to talk, so tell me anything.). soudan ni noru is the friend's side. Sora offers it in line 3 (boku de yokattara, soudan noru yo) and line 17 (itsudemo soudan ni noru yo), and Mai thanks him for it in line 18 (soudan ni notte moratte, yokatta). It means hearing someone out and helping them think it through, so adobaisu suru (passing on tips) misses the point. Don't answer with soudan ga aru: that's the side with the problem, the way Mai and Yuki say it (lines 2 and 12). In casual speech the ni often drops (soudan noru yo).
5. Open with honne o iu to (to be honest), the way Mai does, then say what you really think and soften it: chotto hen kamo (it might be a bit odd), or say you like her old one better. For example: *etto, honne o iu to, chotto hen kamo.* (Um, to be honest, it looks a bit odd.); *honne o iu to, mae no boushi no hou ga suki kana.* (Honestly? I think I like your old hat better.). honne is what you really feel, behind the polite front (tatemae), and honne o iu to opens a frank admission. Mai uses it in line 6 (honne o iu to, anmari ikitakunai n da), and so does Yuki's message in line 12 (honne o iu to, kyanpu nigate na n da). Sora uses the other everyday form to invite it: honne itte ii yo (line 5) and honne, itte mitara? (line 9). Yuki's kawaii desho? is fishing for the polite answer, and that's why honne fits. shoujiki ni iu to also means 'to be honest', but it's just being frank, not dropping a polite front. Soften what comes next the way Mai does with anmari, using chotto, kamo or kana.

| # | Speaker | Romaji | English |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Sora | mai-san, genki nai ne. nanka nayami aru no? | Mai, you look a bit down. Is something on your mind? |
| 2 | Mai | un. sora-kun, ima ii? chotto soudan ga aru n da kedo. | Yeah. Sora, have you got a minute? I could use your advice on something. |
| 3 | Sora | ii yo, ima hima da shi. boku de yokattara, soudan noru yo. | Sure, it's quiet right now anyway. If I can help, I'm all ears. |
| 4 | Mai | arigatou. kondo no shuumatsu, yuki to kyanpu ni iku no. | Thanks. Yuki and I are going camping this weekend. |
| 5 | Sora | hee, ii ne. demo, tanoshimi ja nai no? honne itte ii yo. | Oh, nice. But you're not looking forward to it? You can tell me how you really feel. |
| 6 | Mai | honne o iu to, anmari ikitakunai n da. soto de neru no, nigate de. | To be honest, I don't really want to go. I'm not great at sleeping outdoors. |
| 7 | Sora | sokka. sore, yuki-san ni itta? | I see. Have you told Yuki? |
| 8 | Mai | ienai yo. watashi ga mae ni, kyanpu tanoshisou tte itta kara, yuki ga keikaku tatete kureta no. | I can't! I once said camping sounded fun, so Yuki went and planned it for me. |
| 9 | Sora | demo, iwanai to, shuumatsu zutto soto de neru koto ni naru yo. honne, itte mitara? | But if you don't, you'll be sleeping outdoors all weekend. Why not tell her how you really feel? |
| 10 | Mai | sou da yo ne. a, yuki kara renraku kita. | You're right. Oh, a message from Yuki. |
| 11 | Sora | nante? | What does she say? |
| 12 | Mai | chotto soudan ga aru n da kedo. honne o iu to, kyanpu nigate na n da tte. | "There's something I want to ask you about. To be honest, I'm not really into camping." |
| 13 | Sora | e, yuki-san mo onaji nayami ga atta n da! | What? So Yuki had the same worry! |
| 14 | Mai | un. watashi ga ikitasou datta kara, keikaku tateta n da tte. | Yeah. She says she only planned it because I looked like I wanted to go. |
| 15 | Sora | nan da. jaa, mai-san mo yuki-san mo, ikitakunakatta n da. | Ha! So neither of you actually wanted to go. |
| 16 | Mai | sou mitai. kyanpu wa chuushi ni narisou da kedo, kawari ni yuki to koko ni kuru ne. | Looks like it. The camping trip's probably off, but I'll come here with Yuki instead. |
| 17 | Sora | ii ne, matteru. mata nayami ga attara, itsudemo soudan ni noru yo. | Great, I'll be here. And if anything's on your mind again, I'm always happy to listen. |
| 18 | Mai | arigatou. sora-kun ni soudan ni notte moratte, yokatta. | Thanks, Sora. I'm really glad I talked it over with you. |

</details>

## 89. An hour to kill

*Using your time · Casual dialogue · 18 lines · Kenji, Okaasan*

A weekday afternoon. Kenji hurt himself at football practice, so Okaasan left work early to take him to the local clinic. They've already waited a while when the receptionist says there's about another hour to go, and that they're free to wait outside.

**Collocations:** *jikan o kakeru* (put time into, take time over); *jikan o toru* (set aside time, take up time); *jikan o tsukuru* (make time); *jikan o tsubusu* (kill time).

**Review:** *kega o suru* (get hurt, get injured); *honne o iu* (say what you really think).

### Audio script

| Speaker | Voice |
|---|---|
| Kenji | male, late teens, sleepy, mumbling |
| Okaasan | female, 50s, warm, motherly |

| # | Speaker | Japanese | Kana (if the voice misreads a kanji) |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Kenji | え、まだ一時間もかかるの？ | え、まだいちじかんもかかるの？ |
| 2 | Okaasan | しょうがないのよ。ここの先生、一人一人ちゃんと時間かけて診てくれるから。 | しょうがないのよ。ここのせんせい、ひとりひとりちゃんとじかんかけてみてくれるから。 |
| 3 | Kenji | 時間かけすぎだよ。ここで一時間も待つの、無理。 | じかんかけすぎだよ。ここでいちじかんもまつの、むり。 |
| 4 | Okaasan | じゃあ、前のカフェで時間潰そうか。 | じゃあ、まえのかふぇでじかんつぶそうか。 |
| 5 | Kenji | いいね。何か食べながら時間潰そう。 | いいね。なにかたべながらじかんつぶそう。 |
| 6 | Okaasan | もう二つ目？怪我してるのに、よく食べるね。 | もうふたつめ？けがしてるのに、よくたべるね。 |
| 7 | Kenji | だって昼、ちょっとしか食べてないんだもん。お母さんは、仕事大丈夫だったの？ | だってひる、ちょっとしかたべてないんだもん。おかあさんは、しごとだいじょうぶだったの？ |
| 8 | Okaasan | うん。今週は忙しいけど、なんとか時間作ったの。 | うん。こんしゅうはいそがしいけど、なんとかじかんつくったの。 |
| 9 | Okaasan | いつも仕事ばっかりで、ケンジとゆっくり話す時間、なかなか取れないしね。 | いつもしごとばっかりで、けんじとゆっくりはなすじかん、なかなかとれないしね。 |
| 10 | Kenji | そっか。なんか、時間取ってくれてありがとう。 | そっか。なんか、じかんとってくれてありがとう。 |
| 11 | Okaasan | 何よ、急に。でも、うれしいわ。 | なによ、きゅうに。でも、うれしいわ。 |
| 12 | Kenji | あ、でも本音言うと、帰りたい。なんかもう痛くないし。 | あ、でもほんねいうと、かえりたい。なんかもういたくないし。 |
| 13 | Okaasan | だめ。せっかく時間作ったんだから、ちゃんと先生に診てもらってから帰るの。 | だめ。せっかくじかんつくったんだから、ちゃんとせんせいにみてもらってからかえるの。 |
| 14 | Kenji | え、じゃあ来週も来てって言われたら？ | え、じゃあらいしゅうもきてっていわれたら？ |
| 15 | Okaasan | 来週なら、また時間作れるから大丈夫。 | らいしゅうなら、またじかんつくれるからだいじょうぶ。 |
| 16 | Kenji | じゃあ、もう一つ食べて、時間潰そうかな。 | じゃあ、もうひとつたべて、じかんつぶそうかな。 |
| 17 | Okaasan | まだ食べるの？ | まだたべるの？ |
| 18 | Kenji | うん。今度は時間かけて、ゆっくり食べる。 | うん。こんどはじかんかけて、ゆっくりたべる。 |

### Questions

1. **What happens while Kenji and Okaasan wait to see the doctor?**  
   nani ga arimashita ka? (何がありましたか？)
   - A. Okaasan couldn't get away from work, so Kenji waited for the doctor on his own.
   - B. With an hour to wait, they killed time at a café. Kenji said it didn't hurt any more and wanted to go home, but Okaasan said he'd see the doctor first, since she'd made the time.
   - C. They gave up on the wait and went home, and Okaasan will make time to bring Kenji back next week.

2. **Okaasan explains why the wait is so long. What does she say about the doctor?**  
   okaasan wa, sensei ni tsuite nan to iimashita ka? (お母さんは、先生について何と言いましたか？)
   - A. He spends a full hour with every patient.
   - B. He's always running late, so his patients end up waiting.
   - C. He takes his time and looks at each patient properly.

3. **Your manager Tanaka-san stayed after work to go through your presentation with you, even though his week is packed. When you're done, he says, "jaa, kyou wa kono gurai ni shimashou ka." Thank him for making time for you, using jikan o toru.** *(say it)*  
   jaa, kyou wa kono gurai ni shimashou ka. (じゃあ、今日はこのぐらいにしましょうか。)

4. **Kenta asks, "konshuu, issho ni nanika tabe ni ikanai?" Your week is packed, but next week looks better. Tell him you can make time next week.** *(say it)*  
   konshuu, issho ni nanika tabe ni ikanai? (今週、一緒に何か食べに行かない？)

5. **There's another hour to wait. What did Okaasan suggest?**  
   okaasan wa nan to iimashita ka? (お母さんは何と言いましたか？)
   - A. *mae no kafe de jikan tsubusou ka* (Shall we kill some time at the café across the road?)
   - B. *mae no kafe de jikan korosou ka* (Shall we murder some time at the café across the road?)
   - C. *mae no kafe de jikan kakeyou ka* (Shall we put some time into the café across the road?)

### New words

- **kafe** (カフェ): café
- **sensei** (先生): doctor (also teacher)
- **hitori hitori** (一人一人): each person, one by one

<details>
<summary>Answers and transcript</summary>

1. **B**. Line 1: there's still an hour to wait (mada ichijikan mo kakaru). In lines 4-5 they go to the café across the road to kill time. Line 8: Okaasan managed to make time in a busy week. In line 12 Kenji says it doesn't hurt any more and he wants to go home. In line 13 she says no: sekkaku jikan tsukutta n da kara, the doctor looks at it first. A is wrong: Okaasan is right there with him and says she made the time (line 8). C is wrong: she won't go home (line 13). Next week only comes up in case the doctor asks Kenji to come back (lines 14-15).
2. **C**. Line 2: 'koko no sensei, hitori hitori chanto jikan kakete mite kureru kara'. Jikan o kakeru is choosing to put time into something, here each patient (hitori hitori), properly (chanto). Kenji grumbles jikan kakesugi da yo (line 3, he takes way too long), and at the end he'll eat his snack slowly: jikan kakete, yukkuri taberu (line 18). A mixes it up with line 1: the hour is how long the wait still takes, mada ichijikan mo kakaru, and kakaru is how long something takes. It isn't time per patient. B is wrong: nobody says he's late. The wait is long because he's thorough.
3. Thank him politely with jikan o toru: ojikan totte itadaite, arigatou gozaimashita (thank you for making time for me). To apologise for taking up his time, use the causative: ojikan torasete shimatte sumimasen. For example: *hai. kyou wa ojikan totte itadaite, arigatou gozaimashita.* (Yes. Thank you for making time for me today.); *oisogashii tokoro, ojikan torasete shimatte sumimasen. arigatou gozaimashita.* (Sorry to take up your time when you're so busy. Thank you.). Kenji says it to Okaasan in line 10: nanka, jikan totte kurete arigatou. She has just said she hardly ever gets time to talk with him (line 9, nakanaka torenai). Jikan o toru is setting time aside, and it's the usual way to thank someone for theirs. With your manager, make it polite with o- and itadaite: ojikan totte itadaite. The apology uses the causative: ojikan torasete (making you take the time). English 'take' can mislead you, though: how long something takes is kakaru, as in mada ichijikan mo kakaru (line 1). Ojikan tsukutte itadaite would also work, if you want to stress that he squeezed you in.
4. Use jikan o tsukuru in the potential: raishuu nara jikan tsukureru yo (I can make time next week). Saying konshuu wa chotto isogashii kedo first softens the no. For example: *konshuu wa chotto isogashii kedo, raishuu nara jikan tsukureru yo.* (This week's a bit busy, but I can make time next week.); *gomen, konshuu wa muri. raishuu nara, nantoka jikan tsukuru kara.* (Sorry, this week's impossible. I'll find time next week somehow.). Okaasan uses it three times: nantoka jikan tsukutta (line 8, she managed to make time in a busy week), sekkaku jikan tsukutta n da kara (line 13, I went to the trouble of making time) and raishuu nara, mata jikan tsukureru (line 15, I can make time again next week). 'Make time' really is tsukuru, and as in English it means squeezing time out of a full schedule. Jikan o toru (lines 9-10) is setting it aside. The potential, jikan tsukureru, is how you promise to fit someone in. With a friend, casual is right.
5. **A**. Line 4: 'jaa, mae no kafe de jikan tsubusou ka'. Kenji answers nanika tabenagara jikan tsubusou (line 5), and later mou hitotsu tabete, jikan tsubusou kana (line 16). Killing time is tsubusu, 'squash': you squash the empty time flat. B is a word-for-word copy of 'kill time'. Korosu is for killing living things, so jikan o korosu sounds like a murder plot. C is wrong: jikan o kakeru is putting time into something on purpose, like the doctor with each patient (line 2). Here they just want to fill a wait.

## 90. The idea from the bath

*Getting work done · Mixed dialogue · 17 lines · Tanaka-san, Yuki, Mai*

Monday afternoon at the office. On Friday, Tanaka-san asked Yuki for an idea for the next campaign, and he wants it today. Her coworker and friend Mai (Kimura-san at work) sits at the next desk.

**Collocations:** *atama o tsukau* (use your head; make you think); *kekka o dasu* (get results, deliver results); *memo o toru* (take notes, write it down); *aidea ga ukabu* (an idea comes to mind); *hanko o osu* (stamp your seal).

**Review:** *kekka ga deru* (the results come out); *soudan ga aru* (want to ask your advice).

### Audio script

| Tanaka-san | male, 40s, calm and kind, polite office speech |
| Yuki | female, late 20s, bright and friendly |
| Mai | female, late 20s, warm, a little shy |

| 13 | Mai | これなら、きっと田中さんもハンコ押してくれるし、結果も出せるよ。 | これなら、きっとたなかさんもはんこおしてくれるし、けっかもだせるよ。 |

### Questions

1. **What happened with Yuki's campaign idea?**  
   nani ga arimashita ka? (何がありましたか？)
   - A. Yuki couldn't come up with anything, so Mai gave Tanaka-san one of her own ideas, and he approved it.
   - B. Yuki forgot the idea she had in the bath, but Mai had written it down when Yuki phoned her, and Tanaka-san approved it.
   - C. Yuki remembered her bath idea after a short break, but Tanaka-san said it needed more work before he'd stamp it.

2. **At lunch, Mai hands you her phone with a game on it and says, "kore, saikin hamatteru geemu. yatte miru?" You play a round. Tell her it really makes you think.** *(say it)*  
   kore, saikin hamatteru geemu. yatte miru? (これ、最近ハマってるゲーム。やってみる？)

3. **At the end, Tanaka-san tells Yuki and Mai what he wants from them this time. What did he say?**  
   tanaka-san wa saigo ni nan to iimashita ka? (田中さんは最後に何と言いましたか？)
   - A. *kondo koso kekka o dashite kudasai ne* (This time, make sure you get results.)
   - B. *kondo koso kekka o moratte kudasai ne* (This time, please go and receive the results.)
   - C. *kondo koso kekka o totte kudasai ne* (This time, please take the results.)

4. **Tanaka-san comes to your desk and says, "raishuu no kaigi, jikan ga kawatta node, ima iimasu ne." Ask him to wait a moment while you write it down.** *(say it)*  
   raishuu no kaigi, jikan ga kawatta node, ima iimasu ne. (来週の会議、時間が変わったので、今言いますね。)

5. **Mai says everyone wants to get Tanaka-san something for his birthday, and asks you, "nee, nani ga ii to omou?" You've thought about it, but nothing comes to mind. Tell her.** *(say it)*  
   tanaka-san no tanjoubi, minna de nanika ageyou tte. nee, nani ga ii to omou? (田中さんの誕生日、みんなで何かあげようって。ねえ、何がいいと思う？)

6. **Yuki brings her idea to Tanaka-san. What did he do?**  
   tanaka-san wa, yuki-san no aidea o dou shimashita ka? (田中さんは、ユキさんのアイデアをどうしましたか？)
   - A. He asked Yuki to put her own seal on it first, before he read it.
   - B. He gave it a fun 'good job' stamp, like a teacher, but hasn't decided yet.
   - C. He approved it: his seal on the plan means it can go ahead.

### New words

- **kyanpeen** (キャンペーン): campaign (sales promotion)
- **okyakusan** (お客さん): customer

<details>
<summary>Answers and transcript</summary>

1. **B**. Line 3: Tanaka-san wants Yuki's campaign idea by the end of the day. Line 6: a great idea came to Yuki in the bath last night, but she has forgotten it. Lines 9-11: she phoned Mai after her bath, and Mai wrote the idea down just in case. Line 15: Tanaka-san likes it and presses his hanko on it, which means he approves. A is wrong: the idea was Yuki's own. Mai only wrote it down, and Yuki tells Tanaka-san so (line 16). C is wrong: Yuki never remembers it by herself. She reads it in Mai's notes (line 12), and Tanaka-san approves it on the spot.
2. Say the game makes you think with atama o tsukau, casually atama tsukau, with kekkou or metcha for 'really': kono geemu, kekkou atama tsukau ne. For example: *kono geemu, kekkou atama tsukau ne.* (This game really makes you think.); *muzukashii! kore, metcha atama tsukau ne.* (It's hard! This really gets your brain working.). Yuki has been racking her brains since the morning (line 4, asa kara zutto atama tsukatteru), and Mai tells her she's overdoing it (line 5, atama tsukaisugi da yo). A game or a job that makes you think is one that 'uses your head': atama o tsukau, in casual speech usually without o. Don't mix it up with atama ga ii (be clever), or with ki o tsukau, which means being considerate. With a friend, casual is right.
3. **A**. Line 17: 'futari de, kondo koso kekka o dashite kudasai ne'. Yuki used it in line 2 (kondo koso, kekka o dashitai desu ne), and Mai in line 13 (kekka mo daseru yo). Results you achieve are something you put out yourself: kekka o dasu. In line 1 Tanaka-san used the other verb, kekka ga demashita. There the results came out by themselves, meaning they were announced. B is wrong: morau means being handed something, so kekka o morau only fits getting a results sheet. C is wrong: toru works in ten o toru (get a score) and memo o toru, but results aren't 'taken'. You deliver them with dasu.
4. Ask him to wait while you take a note, with memo o toru in polite form: chotto matte kudasai, memo o torimasu. He's your manager, so stay polite. For example: *a, chotto matte kudasai. ima memo o torimasu.* (Oh, just a moment, please. I'll write it down.); *sumimasen, memo o torimasu node, chotto matte kudasai.* (Sorry, let me take a note. Just a moment, please.). Mai makes a point of noting ideas down the moment they come (line 7, sugu memo o toru you ni shiteru), and she wrote Yuki's idea down during the call (line 11, ichiou memo totta). Yuki tells Tanaka-san that Kimura-san took it down for her (line 16, memo o totte kureta). As in English, you 'take' notes: memo o toru. Memo suru means the same and is just as common. A note you leave for someone is memo o nokosu. With your manager, use torimasu and matte kudasai.
5. Say no good idea comes to mind with aidea ga ukabu in the negative: zenzen ii aidea ukabanai. With Mai, casual is right. For example: *uun, zenzen ii aidea ukabanai.* (Hmm, I can't come up with any good ideas at all.); *nan darou. mada ii aidea ga ukabanai n da yo ne.* (Good question. No good idea has come to me yet.). Yuki complains that no good idea comes to her (line 4, zenzen ii aidea ga ukabanai), though a great one came to her in the bath (line 6, ukanda). Ideas float up into your mind on their own, so the idea is the subject with ga: aidea ga ukabu, and casual speech often drops the ga. It is mostly heard in the negative, like this. Ukabu reports an idea coming, or not coming, to mind, as in sugoi aidea ukanda! (line 11). To offer an idea, 'I've got an idea!', people say ii kangae ga aru! or omoitsuita! With Mai, casual is right.
6. **C**. Line 15: 'omoshiroi desu ne, kore. jaa, hanko o oshimasu ne.' In a Japanese office a manager approves a plan by pressing his personal seal (hanko) on it, so this means 'yes, go ahead'. He promised it in line 3 (ii aidea nara, sugu hanko o oshimasu kara), and Mai predicted it in line 13. A is wrong: nobody asks Yuki for her own seal. B is wrong: a fun rubber stamp, like a teacher's 'well done', is a sutanpu. A hanko is a personal seal, and you press it: hanko o osu.

| # | Speaker | Romaji | English |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Tanaka-san | yuki-san, sengetsu no kyanpeen, kekka ga demashita yo. amari yokunakatta desu ne. | Yuki-san, last month's campaign results are in. I'm afraid they weren't very good. |
| 2 | Yuki | sou desu ka. kondo koso, kekka o dashitai desu ne. | I see. This time I really want to get results. |
| 3 | Tanaka-san | kin'youbi ni tanonda aidea, kyoujuu ni onegai shimasu ne. ii aidea nara, sugu hanko o oshimasu kara. | The idea I asked you for on Friday: I'd like it by the end of today. If it's a good one, I'll put my stamp on it straight away. |
| 4 | Yuki | mai, chotto soudan ga aru n da kedo. asa kara zutto atama tsukatteru noni, zenzen ii aidea ga ukabanai no. | Mai, can I ask your advice? I've been racking my brains all morning, and I can't come up with a single good idea. |
| 5 | Mai | atama tsukaisugi da yo. chotto yasundara? | You're thinking too hard. Why not take a little break? |

| 7 | Mai | e, memo toranakatta no? watashi wa, aidea ga ukandara, sugu memo o toru you ni shiteru yo. | What, you didn't write it down? Whenever an idea comes to me, I make a point of writing it down right away. |

| 10 | Yuki | e, sou datta? tsukaretete, zenzen oboetenai. | Huh, did I? I was so tired, I don't remember at all. |

| 15 | Tanaka-san | omoshiroi desu ne, kore. jaa, hanko o oshimasu ne. | This is interesting. All right, I'll put my stamp on it. |
| 16 | Yuki | arigatou gozaimasu! jitsu wa, kimura-san ga memo o totte kureta n desu. | Thank you! Actually, Kimura-san wrote it down for me. |

## 91. Just a yawn

*When it hits you · Casual dialogue · 16 lines · Yuki, Kenta*

A Saturday night outside a concert hall. Yuki has loved a singer for ten years and has just seen her live for the first time. Her old friend Kenta came along on her spare ticket, though he'd said he didn't know any of the songs.

**Collocations:** *namida ga deru* (tear up, eyes water); *torihada ga tatsu* (get goosebumps); *jikkan ga waku* (it sinks in, feels real).

**Review:** *honne o iu* (say what you really think).

### Audio script

| Speaker | Voice |
|---|---|
| Yuki | female, late 20s, bright and friendly |
| Kenta | male, late 20s, relaxed and easygoing |

| # | Speaker | Japanese | Kana (if the voice misreads a kanji) |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Yuki | ああ、すごかった！最後の歌、鳥肌立った！ | ああ、すごかった！さいごのうた、とりはだたった！ |
| 2 | Kenta | わかる。ほら、僕もまだ鳥肌立ってる。 | わかる。ほら、ぼくもまだとりはだたってる。 |
| 3 | Yuki | でしょ？あの人の歌、全然知らないって言ってたのに。 | でしょ？あのひとのうた、ぜんぜんしらないっていってたのに。 |
| 4 | Kenta | うん、でもいい歌ばっかりだった。ユキは最初から泣いてたね。 | うん、でもいいうたばっかりだった。ゆきはさいしょからないてたね。 |
| 5 | Yuki | だって、十年待って、やっと会えたんだよ。まだ実感湧かない。 | だって、じゅうねんまって、やっとあえたんだよ。まだじっかんわかない。 |
| 6 | Kenta | 十年か。実感湧かないのもわかるよ。 | じゅうねんか。じっかんわかないのもわかるよ。 |
| 7 | Yuki | あれ？ケンタも目赤いよ。もしかして、泣いてた？ | あれ？けんたもめあかいよ。もしかして、ないてた？ |
| 8 | Kenta | 泣いてないよ。ちょっとあくびしたら、涙出ただけ。 | ないてないよ。ちょっとあくびしたら、なみだでただけ。 |
| 9 | Yuki | 最後の歌であくび？つまらなかったってこと？ | さいごのうたであくび？つまらなかったってこと？ |
| 10 | Kenta | 違う違う！なんかわかんないけど、急に涙出てきたんだよ。 | ちがうちがう！なんかわかんないけど、きゅうになみだでてきたんだよ。 |
| 11 | Yuki | それ、泣いたって言うんだよ。 | それ、ないたっていうんだよ。 |
| 12 | Kenta | わかったよ。本音言うと、僕もちょっと泣いた。 | わかったよ。ほんねいうと、ぼくもちょっとないた。 |
| 13 | Yuki | やっぱり！ケンタまで泣くほどよかったんだね。 | やっぱり！けんたまでなくほどよかったんだね。 |
| 14 | Kenta | うん、ほんとによかった。ユキは？少しは実感湧いてきた？ | うん、ほんとによかった。ゆきは？すこしはじっかんわいてきた？ |
| 15 | Yuki | うん、やっと湧いてきた。あ、だめ、また涙出そう。 | うん、やっとわいてきた。あ、だめ、またなみだでそう。 |
| 16 | Kenta | やめてよ。僕もまた鳥肌立ってきた。 | やめてよ。ぼくもまたとりはだたってきた。 |

### Questions

1. **What happens after the concert?**  
   nani ga arimashita ka? (何がありましたか？)
   - A. Kenta yawned through the last song because he didn't know it, and Yuki is upset that he found it boring.
   - B. Kenta blames a yawn for his teary eyes, but he ends up admitting the concert made him cry a little.
   - C. Kenta says he didn't cry, and Yuki believes him, since he didn't know any of the songs.

2. **After lunch at the office, you give a big yawn and your eyes water. Mai, at the next desk, looks over and asks, "e, naiteru no? daijoubu?" Tell her you're not crying: your eyes just watered when you yawned.** *(say it)*  
   e, naiteru no? daijoubu? (え、泣いてるの？大丈夫？)

3. **Right after the concert, what does Yuki say about the last song?**  
   yuki-san wa, saigo no uta ni tsuite nan to iimashita ka? (ユキさんは、最後の歌について何と言いましたか？)
   - A. *saigo no uta, torihada moratta!* (I got goosebumps from the last song! (morau: get, as in being given something))
   - B. *saigo no uta, torihada deta!* (Goosebumps came out at the last song! (deru: come out, as tears do))
   - C. *saigo no uta, torihada tatta!* (The last song gave me goosebumps!)

4. **You've just passed an exam you studied for all year. Your manager, Tanaka-san, comes over and says, "shiken, ukatta sou desu ne. omedetou gozaimasu." Thank him, and say it still hasn't sunk in.** *(say it)*  
   shiken, ukatta sou desu ne. omedetou gozaimasu. (試験、受かったそうですね。おめでとうございます。)

### New words

- **naku** (泣く): cry (naiteta = was crying, naita = cried)
- **akubi** (あくび): yawn (akubi suru = to yawn)

<details>
<summary>Answers and transcript</summary>

1. **B**. Lines 7-8: Yuki sees that Kenta's eyes are red too and asks if he was crying. He says no, he just yawned and his eyes watered (akubi shitara, namida deta dake). In line 9 she asks if that means he was bored, and in line 10 he says no, the tears just suddenly came (kyuu ni namida dete kita). Yuki tells him that's called crying (line 11), and in line 12 he admits it: honne iu to, boku mo chotto naita. A is wrong: the yawn was only Kenta's excuse. He says chigau chigau! (line 10), and that the concert was really good (line 14). C is wrong: Yuki doesn't believe him (line 11), and he ends up admitting he cried.
2. Say you're not crying (naitenai yo), and that your eyes only watered because you yawned, with namida ga deru in the past: akubi shitara namida deta dake. In casual speech ga usually drops. For example: *naitenai yo. akubi shitara, namida deta dake.* (I'm not crying. I yawned and my eyes watered, that's all.); *uun, chigau chigau. akubi shitara, namida dete kichatta.* (No, no, that's not it. I yawned, and my eyes started watering.). Kenta uses it to deny crying: akubi shitara, namida deta dake (line 8). Then he gives himself away with kyuu ni namida dete kita (line 10, the tears just started coming), and at the end Yuki says mata namida desou (line 15, I'm going to tear up again). namida ga deru is tears coming out on their own: from a yawn, onions or laughing, or when something moves you. naku is crying from emotion. That's why Mai asks naiteru no?, and why Yuki tells Kenta sore, naita tte iu n da yo (line 11): tears because a song moved you count as crying. A yawn doesn't, so say naitenai and namida deta dake. With a friend like Mai, casual is right, and ga usually drops.
3. **C**. Line 1: 'saigo no uta, torihada tatta!' In Japanese, goosebumps stand up: torihada ga tatsu, and in casual speech ga usually drops. Kenta uses it twice: hora, boku mo mada torihada tatteru (line 2, look, I've still got goosebumps too) and boku mo mata torihada tatte kita (line 16). A is wrong: morau is 'get' as in being given something, so torihada moratta copies English 'got goosebumps' word for word. B is wrong: deru is the verb for tears (namida deta, line 8), but goosebumps don't come out, they stand up. Each reaction keeps its own verb: namida ga deru, torihada ga tatsu.
4. Thank him (arigatou gozaimasu), then say it hasn't sunk in yet with jikkan ga waku in the negative: mada jikkan ga wakanai desu. With your manager, keep ga and add desu. With friends it's just mada jikkan wakanai. For example: *arigatou gozaimasu. demo, mada jikkan ga wakanai desu.* (Thank you. It still hasn't sunk in, though.); *arigatou gozaimasu. jitsu wa, mada zenzen jikkan ga wakanai n desu.* (Thank you. Honestly, it hasn't sunk in at all yet.). After finally seeing the singer, Yuki says mada jikkan wakanai (line 5, it still hasn't sunk in). Kenta answers jikkan wakanai no mo wakaru yo (line 6), and later asks sukoshi wa jikkan waite kita? (line 14, has it started to sink in?). jikkan is the real feel of something, and waku is a feeling welling up, so it's mostly said in the negative after big news like passing an exam, a new job or a baby. Don't say mada wakaranai or rikai shite nai, which sound as if you don't understand the news. Listen for one n: wakanai (doesn't well up, from waku) against wakannai (I don't get it), which Kenta says in line 10 (nanka wakannai kedo). With your manager, keep ga and add desu: mada jikkan ga wakanai desu.

| # | Speaker | Romaji | English |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Yuki | aa, sugokatta! saigo no uta, torihada tatta! | Wow, that was amazing! The last song gave me goosebumps! |
| 2 | Kenta | wakaru. hora, boku mo mada torihada tatteru. | I know. Look, I've still got goosebumps too. |
| 3 | Yuki | desho? ano hito no uta, zenzen shiranai tte itteta noni. | Right? And you said you didn't know any of her songs. |
| 4 | Kenta | un, demo ii uta bakkari datta. yuki wa saisho kara naiteta ne. | Yeah, but they were all great songs. You were crying from the very start. |
| 5 | Yuki | datte, juu nen matte, yatto aeta n da yo. mada jikkan wakanai. | Well, I waited ten years, and I finally got to see her. It still hasn't sunk in. |
| 6 | Kenta | juu nen ka. jikkan wakanai no mo wakaru yo. | Ten years, huh. I can see why it hasn't sunk in. |
| 7 | Yuki | are? kenta mo me akai yo. moshikashite, naiteta? | Huh? Your eyes are red too, Kenta. Were you crying, by any chance? |
| 8 | Kenta | naitenai yo. chotto akubi shitara, namida deta dake. | I wasn't crying. I yawned, and my eyes watered, that's all. |
| 9 | Yuki | saigo no uta de akubi? tsumaranakatta tte koto? | A yawn, during the last song? So you were bored? |
| 10 | Kenta | chigau chigau! nanka wakannai kedo, kyuu ni namida dete kita n da yo. | No, no! I don't know why, but the tears just suddenly came. |
| 11 | Yuki | sore, naita tte iu n da yo. | That's called crying. |
| 12 | Kenta | wakatta yo. honne iu to, boku mo chotto naita. | Fine. If I'm honest, I cried a bit too. |
| 13 | Yuki | yappari! kenta made naku hodo yokatta n da ne. | I knew it! So it was good enough to make even you cry. |
| 14 | Kenta | un, honto ni yokatta. yuki wa? sukoshi wa jikkan waite kita? | Yeah, it was really good. What about you? Has it started to sink in? |
| 15 | Yuki | un, yatto waite kita. a, dame, mata namida desou. | Yeah, it's finally sinking in. Oh no, I'm going to tear up again. |
| 16 | Kenta | yamete yo. boku mo mata torihada tatte kita. | Don't. Now I've got goosebumps again too. |

</details>

## 92. Hatsumoude in April

*Seasonal outings · Casual dialogue · 17 lines · Kenta, Sora*

A Saturday in early April. Flatmates Kenta and Sora are walking to a cherry-blossom picnic in the park, where Yuki and the others are waiting, and pass a small shrine on the way.

**Collocations:** *ohanami ni iku* (go cherry-blossom viewing); *hatsumoude ni iku* (make the year's first shrine visit); *omikuji o hiku* (draw a fortune slip).

**Review:** *hana ga saku* (flowers bloom, come out).

### Audio script

| Speaker | Voice |
|---|---|
| Kenta | male, late 20s, relaxed and easygoing |
| Sora | male, mid 20s, light voice, playful |

| # | Speaker | Japanese | Kana (if the voice misreads a kanji) |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Sora | うわー、花、めっちゃ咲いてる！お花見行くの、久しぶりだなあ。 | うわー、はな、めっちゃさいてる！おはなみいくの、ひさしぶりだなあ。 |
| 2 | Kenta | 去年は二人とも忙しくて、お花見行けなかったもんね。 | きょねんはふたりともいそがしくて、おはなみいけなかったもんね。 |
| 3 | Sora | ねえ、ここ神社でしょ？おみくじ引いていこうよ。 | ねえ、ここじんじゃでしょ？おみくじひいていこうよ。 |
| 4 | Kenta | おみくじは、いいや。初詣のとき、大凶だったから。 | おみくじは、いいや。はつもうでのとき、だいきょうだったから。 |
| 5 | Sora | 大凶！？そんなの、ほんとに入ってるんだ。 | だいきょう！？そんなの、ほんとにはいってるんだ。 |
| 6 | Kenta | 入ってるよ。ユキと初詣行って、ユキは大吉、こっちは大凶。 | はいってるよ。ゆきとはつもうでいって、ゆきはだいきち、こっちはだいきょう。 |
| 7 | Sora | あはは、最悪じゃん。でも僕なんて、今年まだ初詣行ってないよ。 | あはは、さいあくじゃん。でもぼくなんて、ことしまだはつもうでいってないよ。 |
| 8 | Kenta | え、もう4月だよ。 | え、もうしがつだよ。 |
| 9 | Sora | だから、今日が僕の初詣。おみくじも引かなきゃ。 | だから、きょうがぼくのはつもうで。おみくじもひかなきゃ。 |
| 10 | Kenta | 遅すぎるでしょ。まあ、二回も大凶は出ないか。 | おそすぎるでしょ。まあ、にかいもだいきょうはでないか。 |
| 11 | Sora | 引いた？じゃあ、一緒に見よう。 | ひいた？じゃあ、いっしょにみよう。 |
| 12 | Kenta | あ、大吉！やった、大吉だ！ | あ、だいきち！やった、だいきちだ！ |
| 13 | Sora | え、うそ。僕、大凶なんだけど。 | え、うそ。ぼく、だいきょうなんだけど。 |
| 14 | Kenta | あはは！4月の初詣は、やっぱりだめだね。 | あはは！しがつのはつもうでは、やっぱりだめだね。 |
| 15 | Sora | もう、これ結んで、早くお花見行こう。ユキたち、待ってるし。 | もう、これむすんで、はやくおはなみいこう。ゆきたち、まってるし。 |
| 16 | Kenta | うん。来年はちゃんと1月に初詣行こうね。 | うん。らいねんはちゃんといちがつにはつもうでいこうね。 |
| 17 | Sora | 行く。でも、おみくじはもう引かない。 | いく。でも、おみくじはもうひかない。 |

### Questions

1. **What happened at the shrine?**  
   nani ga arimashita ka? (何がありましたか？)
   - A. Sora drew great luck on his very late first shrine visit, and Kenta drew very bad luck for the second time.
   - B. Kenta drew great luck this time, but Sora, who called today his first shrine visit of the year, drew very bad luck.
   - C. Kenta wouldn't draw another fortune after his bad one at New Year, so only Sora drew one, and he got very bad luck.

2. **A week before the picnic, Sora comes home and says, "hana, mou saiteru tte." Suggest going cherry-blossom viewing at the weekend.** *(say it)*  
   hana, mou saiteru tte. (花、もう咲いてるって。)

3. **Right before Kenta points out that it's already April, what does Sora tell him?**  
   sora wa nan to iimashita ka? (ソラは何と言いましたか？)
   - A. He has never been to this shrine before.
   - B. He hasn't been cherry-blossom viewing yet this year.
   - C. He hasn't made his first shrine visit of the year yet.

4. **It's 2 January and you've just got back from the shrine. Kenta asks, "hatsumoude, dou datta?" Tell him you drew a fortune slip, and what you got.** *(say it)*  
   hatsumoude, dou datta? (初詣、どうだった？)

### New words

- **jinja** (神社): shrine
- **daikichi** (大吉): great luck (the best fortune)
- **daikyou** (大凶): very bad luck (the worst fortune)
- **musubu** (結ぶ): tie (a bad fortune is tied up at the shrine and left)

<details>
<summary>Answers and transcript</summary>

1. **B**. Lines 4-6: Kenta got daikyou (very bad luck) at New Year. Line 9: Sora hasn't been to a shrine yet this year, so today counts as his hatsumoude. Lines 10-13: Kenta draws again too and gets daikichi (great luck), and Sora gets daikyou. A swaps the two results; C is wrong because Kenta does draw again (line 10: nikai mo daikyou wa denai ka).
2. Invite him with ohanami ni iku in a let's-go form: ohanami ikou, or ohanami ikanai? (casual speech drops ni). Say ohanami, with the o-, not plain hanami. For example: *honto? jaa, shuumatsu ohanami ikou yo.* (Really? Then let's go cherry-blossom viewing this weekend.); *ii ne. shuumatsu, yuki-tachi to ohanami ikanai?* (Nice. Want to go cherry-blossom viewing with Yuki and the others this weekend?). Lines 1, 2 and 15: ohanami iku no, ohanami ikenakatta, hayaku ohanami ikou. In speech it is nearly always ohanami, with the polite o-, and ni is usually dropped. ikou (let's go) and ikanai? (want to go?) are the everyday ways to invite. An ohanami is a picnic under the trees as much as looking at them.
3. **C**. Line 7: kotoshi mada hatsumoude ittenai yo. hatsumoude is the year's first visit to a shrine or temple, normally made in the first days of January, which is why Kenta says it's already April and teases him about an April hatsumoude (line 14). A reads hatsu 'first' as 'first time ever'; B is ohanami, the outing they're on their way to, not the one Sora missed.
4. Say you drew one with omikuji o hiku, usually omikuji hiita, or omikuji hiitara followed by the result (daikichi datta). Drawing a fortune is hiku, 'pull', not kaku. For example: *tanoshikatta yo. omikuji hiitara, daikichi datta!* (It was fun. I drew a fortune slip and got great luck!); *hito ga ippai datta kedo, omikuji hiita yo. daikichi!* (It was packed, but I drew a fortune slip. Great luck!). Lines 3, 9 and 17: omikuji hiite ikou yo, omikuji mo hikanakya, omikuji wa mou hikanai. Drawing lots or a fortune is hiku, 'pull', the same verb as kuji o hiku. English 'draw' pulls toward kaku, but that is drawing a picture, and omikuji o toru isn't said. It sounds like piano o hiku, but that is a different verb.

| # | Speaker | Romaji | English |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Sora | uwaa, hana, metcha saiteru! ohanami iku no, hisashiburi da naa. | Wow, the blossoms are all out! It's been ages since I went cherry-blossom viewing. |
| 2 | Kenta | kyonen wa futari tomo isogashikute, ohanami ikenakatta mon ne. | Last year we were both too busy to go. |
| 3 | Sora | nee, koko jinja desho? omikuji hiite ikou yo. | Hey, this is a shrine, isn't it? Let's draw fortunes before we go on. |
| 4 | Kenta | omikuji wa, ii ya. hatsumoude no toki, daikyou datta kara. | I'll pass on the fortunes. At New Year I got very bad luck. |
| 5 | Sora | daikyou!? sonna no, honto ni haitteru n da. | Very bad luck!? So they really do put those in! |
| 6 | Kenta | haitteru yo. yuki to hatsumoude itte, yuki wa daikichi, kotchi wa daikyou. | They do. I went to the shrine with Yuki at New Year. She got great luck, I got very bad luck. |
| 7 | Sora | ahaha, saiaku jan. demo boku nante, kotoshi mada hatsumoude ittenai yo. | Ha ha, that's the worst. But me, I haven't even made my New Year shrine visit yet. |
| 8 | Kenta | e, mou shigatsu da yo. | What? It's April already. |
| 9 | Sora | dakara, kyou ga boku no hatsumoude. omikuji mo hikanakya. | Exactly, so today's my first shrine visit of the year. I have to draw a fortune too. |
| 10 | Kenta | ososugiru desho. maa, nikai mo daikyou wa denai ka. | That's way too late. Well, I can't get very bad luck twice, can I. |
| 11 | Sora | hiita? jaa, issho ni miyou. | Got yours? OK, let's look together. |
| 12 | Kenta | a, daikichi! yatta, daikichi da! | Oh, great luck! Yes! Great luck! |
| 13 | Sora | e, uso. boku, daikyou na n da kedo. | What, no way. I got very bad luck. |
| 14 | Kenta | ahaha! shigatsu no hatsumoude wa, yappari dame da ne. | Ha ha! Guess a New Year visit in April doesn't count. |
| 15 | Sora | mou, kore musunde, hayaku ohanami ikou. yuki-tachi, matteru shi. | Ugh. I'll tie this one up here, and let's get to the picnic. Yuki and the others are waiting. |
| 16 | Kenta | un. rainen wa chanto ichigatsu ni hatsumoude ikou ne. | Yeah. Next year, let's do the shrine visit properly, in January. |
| 17 | Sora | iku. demo, omikuji wa mou hikanai. | I'm in. But I'm never drawing a fortune again. |

</details>

## 93. Hands up by mistake

*Moving your body · Casual dialogue · 18 lines · Yuki, Mai*

At the office, just after a team meeting. Mai stops by Yuki's desk.

**Collocations:** *te o ageru* (raise your hand); *me o tojiru* (close your eyes); *chikara o nuku* (relax (your body), loosen up).

**Review:** *aidea ga ukabu* (an idea comes to mind).

### Audio script

| Speaker | Voice |
|---|---|
| Yuki | female, late 20s, bright and friendly |
| Mai | female, late 20s, warm, a little shy |

| # | Speaker | Japanese | Kana (if the voice misreads a kanji) |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Mai | ユキ、さっきの会議で手挙げてたけど、ほんとにやるの？ | ゆき、さっきのかいぎでてあげてたけど、ほんとにやるの？ |
| 2 | Yuki | え、何を？肩が痛くて、ちょっと手を上に挙げただけだよ。 | え、なにを？かたがいたくて、ちょっとてをうえにあげただけだよ。 |
| 3 | Mai | ちょうどその時、田中さんが「新しいアイデアがある人は、手を挙げてください」って言ったの。 | ちょうどそのとき、たなかさんが「あたらしいあいであがあるひとは、てをあげてください」っていったの。 |
| 4 | Yuki | うそ、聞いてなかった。じゃあ、来週の会議でアイデア話すの、私？ | うそ、きいてなかった。じゃあ、らいしゅうのかいぎであいであはなすの、わたし？ |
| 5 | Mai | うん。田中さん、すごくうれしそうだったよ。 | うん。たなかさん、すごくうれしそうだったよ。 |
| 6 | Yuki | どうしよう。何もアイデアが浮かばない。 | どうしよう。なにもあいであがうかばない。 |
| 7 | Mai | まあまあ。肩にすごく力入ってるよ。ちょっと力抜いて。 | まあまあ。かたにすごくちからはいってるよ。ちょっとちからぬいて。 |
| 8 | Yuki | 力抜いてるつもりなんだけど。 | ちからぬいてるつもりなんだけど。 |
| 9 | Mai | じゃあ、目閉じて。で、手を上に挙げて。 | じゃあ、めとじて。で、てをうえにあげて。 |
| 10 | Yuki | こう？ | こう？ |
| 11 | Mai | そう。そのまま肩に力入れて。はい、力抜いて。 | そう。そのままかたにちからいれて。はい、ちからぬいて。 |
| 12 | Yuki | あ、なんかいいかも。でも目閉じてると、寝ちゃいそう。 | あ、なんかいいかも。でもめとじてると、ねちゃいそう。 |
| 13 | Mai | 寝ちゃだめだよ。はい、もう一回。 | ねちゃだめだよ。はい、もういっかい。 |
| 14 | Yuki | あっ！目閉じてたら、アイデア浮かんだ！ | あっ！めとじてたら、あいであうかんだ！ |
| 15 | Mai | え、何？ | え、なに？ |
| 16 | Yuki | 会議の前に、みんなでこれやるのはどう？ちょっと力抜くだけで、全然違うよ。 | かいぎのまえに、みんなでこれやるのはどう？ちょっとちからぬくだけで、ぜんぜんちがうよ。 |
| 17 | Mai | いいね！それなら、もう間違えて手を挙げる人もいないしね。 | いいね！それなら、もうまちがえててをあげるひともいないしね。 |
| 18 | Yuki | もう、マイ！ | もう、まい！ |

### Questions

1. **What happened, and how did it turn out?**  
   nani ga arimashita ka? (何がありましたか？)
   - A. Yuki put her hand up on purpose to impress Tanaka-san, and Mai helped her choose the best of her ideas.
   - B. Yuki stretched at just the wrong moment and ended up volunteering, then a stretch with Mai gave her an idea for the meeting.
   - C. Mai put her hand up by mistake in the meeting, so Yuki taught her a stretch to calm her nerves before her talk.

2. **Mai is organising drinks for the whole team on Friday, and everyone's in the room. She says, "kin'youbi, minna de nomi ni iku n da kedo, dare ga kuru ka wakannai n da yo ne." Help her out: ask the people who want to go to put their hands up.** *(say it)*  
   kin'youbi, minna de nomi ni iku n da kedo, dare ga kuru ka wakannai n da yo ne. (金曜日、みんなで飲みに行くんだけど、誰が来るかわかんないんだよね。)

3. **When Mai starts Yuki on the stretch, what's the first thing she tells her to do?**  
   mai wa yuki ni, mazu nan to iimashita ka? (マイはユキに、まず何と言いましたか？)
   - A. *me shimete* (Shut your eyes.)
   - B. *me akete* (Open your eyes.)
   - C. *me tojite* (Close your eyes.)

4. **It's the night before the meeting. Yuki's shoulders are up around her ears, and she says, "ashita no kaigi, dou shiyou. umaku hanaseru kana." Tell her to relax her shoulders, using chikara o nuku.** *(say it)*  
   ashita no kaigi, dou shiyou. umaku hanaseru kana. (明日の会議、どうしよう。うまく話せるかな。)

### New words

- **kaigi** (会議): meeting
- **kata** (肩): shoulder(s)

<details>
<summary>Answers and transcript</summary>

1. **B**. Line 2: Yuki only put her arms up because her shoulders hurt (te o ue ni ageta dake). Line 3: that was just when Tanaka-san asked anyone with a new idea to raise their hand. So now Yuki has to share an idea at next week's meeting (line 4), and she hasn't got one (line 6). Mai talks her through a stretch (lines 9-13), and with her eyes closed an idea comes to her (line 14): everyone doing the stretch before meetings (line 16). A is wrong: Yuki didn't mean to volunteer. She wasn't even listening (line 4), and she had no ideas at all (line 6). C swaps the two: Yuki is the one who put her hand up, and Mai leads the stretch.
2. Ask with te o ageru as a request: name the people, then te agete. Among friends it's iku hito, te agete! To a room of coworkers you can make it polite: ikitai hito wa, te o agete kudasai. For example: *jaa, iku hito, te agete!* (OK, who's coming? Hands up!); *kin'youbi ikitai hito wa, te o agete kudasai.* (If you'd like to come on Friday, please raise your hand.). Tanaka-san's request in line 3 is the classic shape: atarashii aidea ga aru hito wa, te o agete kudasai (anyone with a new idea, please raise your hand). Mai teases Yuki with it in line 17 (machigaete te o ageru hito, people who put their hand up by mistake). The same phrase covers putting your arms up to stretch (line 2, te o ue ni ageta, and line 9, te o ue ni agete). Among friends, o and kudasai drop: iku hito, te agete! Putting your hand back down is te o orosu. Careful with ni and a person: kodomo ni te o ageru means hitting a child.
3. **C**. Line 9: 'jaa, me tojite'. Eyes take tojiru, the verb you also use for closing a book, an umbrella or an app. Yuki says it twice more: me tojiteru to, nechaisou (line 12, with my eyes closed I'll nod off) and me tojitetara, aidea ukanda (line 14). A is wrong: shimeru is for doors, windows and lids, so me o shimeru is the classic learner slip. B is wrong: me akete is the opposite, 'open your eyes', and Yuki's eyes were still open when Mai started. Mai asked her to close them, then to put her arms up (te o ue ni agete). In casual speech you'll also hear me tsubutte for 'close your eyes'.
4. Tell her to relax with chikara o nuku as a request: chikara nuite, or kata no chikara nuite to name her shoulders. With a friend, casual is right and the o usually drops. For example: *daijoubu da yo. kata no chikara nuite.* (You'll be fine. Relax your shoulders.); *kata ni chikara haitteru yo. mou chotto chikara nuite.* (Your shoulders are all tense. Loosen up a bit more.). Mai says it in line 7 (chotto chikara nuite), Yuki thought she already was (line 8, chikara nuiteru tsumori), Mai says it again in line 11, and Yuki's idea rests on it (line 16, chotto chikara nuku dake de, zenzen chigau yo). Nuku means pulling something out, so you pull the strength out of your body. The opposite is chikara o ireru (tense up), which Mai asks for first in line 11 (kata ni chikara irete), and a body that's tense is chikara ga haitteru (line 7). Kata no chikara o nuku also means 'stop trying so hard', which suits a friend worrying about a meeting. Rirakkusu shite is the other everyday way to say relax.

| # | Speaker | Romaji | English |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Mai | yuki, sakki no kaigi de te ageteta kedo, honto ni yaru no? | Yuki, you put your hand up in the meeting just now. Are you really going to do it? |
| 2 | Yuki | e, nani o? kata ga itakute, chotto te o ue ni ageta dake da yo. | Huh? Do what? My shoulders were sore, so I just put my arms up for a second. |
| 3 | Mai | choudo sono toki, tanaka-san ga atarashii aidea ga aru hito wa, te o agete kudasai tte itta no. | Right at that moment, Tanaka-san said, "Anyone with a new idea, please raise your hand." |
| 4 | Yuki | uso, kiitenakatta. jaa, raishuu no kaigi de aidea hanasu no, watashi? | No way, I wasn't listening. So I'm the one presenting an idea at next week's meeting? |
| 5 | Mai | un. tanaka-san, sugoku ureshisou datta yo. | Yep. Tanaka-san looked really pleased. |
| 6 | Yuki | dou shiyou. nanimo aidea ga ukabanai. | What am I going to do? I can't think of a single idea. |
| 7 | Mai | maa maa. kata ni sugoku chikara haitteru yo. chotto chikara nuite. | Easy now. Your shoulders are really tense. Loosen up a bit. |
| 8 | Yuki | chikara nuiteru tsumori na n da kedo. | I thought I was relaxed. |
| 9 | Mai | jaa, me tojite. de, te o ue ni agete. | OK, close your eyes. Now put your arms up. |
| 10 | Yuki | kou? | Like this? |
| 11 | Mai | sou. sono mama kata ni chikara irete. hai, chikara nuite. | That's it. Keep them up and tense your shoulders. OK, now let go. |
| 12 | Yuki | a, nanka ii kamo. demo me tojiteru to, nechaisou. | Oh, that's actually nice. But with my eyes closed, I'm going to nod off. |
| 13 | Mai | necha dame da yo. hai, mou ikkai. | No falling asleep. OK, one more time. |
| 14 | Yuki | a! me tojitetara, aidea ukanda! | Oh! I had my eyes closed and an idea just came to me! |
| 15 | Mai | e, nani? | Really? What? |
| 16 | Yuki | kaigi no mae ni, minna de kore yaru no wa dou? chotto chikara nuku dake de, zenzen chigau yo. | What if we all did this before meetings? Just relaxing a little makes a huge difference. |
| 17 | Mai | ii ne! sore nara, mou machigaete te o ageru hito mo inai shi ne. | Love it! And then nobody will put their hand up by mistake anymore, either. |
| 18 | Yuki | mou, mai! | Oh, stop it, Mai! |

</details>

## 94. Who was that runner?

*Body and fitness · Polite dialogue · 18 lines · Tanaka-san, Mai*

A weekday morning at the office. Tanaka-san, Mai's manager, is putting together a relay team for the company's sports day next month and stops by her desk.

**Collocations:** *me ga warui* (have poor eyesight); *ashi ga hayai* (be a fast runner); *undou shinkei ga ii* (be athletic); *tairyoku ga aru* (have stamina, be fit).

**Review:** *megane o kakeru* (wear / put on glasses); *kami ga nagai* (have long hair).

| Tanaka-san | male, 40s, calm and kind, polite office speech |
| Mai | female, late 20s, warm, a little shy |

| 4 | Mai | え、私ですか？運動神経よくないし、体力もないんですよ。 | え、わたしですか？うんどうしんけいよくないし、たいりょくもないんですよ。 |

| 8 | Mai | 今朝ですか？私、今日はバスで会社まで来ましたけど。 | けさですか？わたし、きょうはばすでかいしゃまできましたけど。 |
| 9 | Tanaka-san | え？じゃあ、あの人は誰だったんでしょう。 | え？じゃあ、あのひとはだれだったんでしょう。 |

### Questions

1. **What happens in this conversation?**  
   nani ga arimashita ka? (何がありましたか？)
   - A. Mai agrees to run in the relay once Tanaka-san says it doesn't take stamina.
   - B. Tanaka-san took someone else for Mai, so he'll ask Yuki to run instead.
   - C. Tanaka-san saw Mai running to work, so he puts her in the relay and Yuki will cheer.

2. **You're squinting at a sign across the street, and Mai asks, "daijoubu? mienai no?" Explain using me ga warui.** *(say it)*  
   daijoubu? mienai no? (大丈夫？見えないの？)

3. **Tanaka-san asks Mai, "kimura-san, ashi ga hayai deshou?" What did he think about her?**  
   tanaka-san wa, kimura-san no koto o dou omotte imashita ka? (田中さんは、木村さんのことをどう思っていましたか？)
   - A. That she always gets to work early.
   - B. That she gets her work done quickly.
   - C. That she's a fast runner.

4. **When Tanaka-san asks Mai to run, how does she describe herself?**  
   kimura-san wa, jibun no koto o nan to iimashita ka? (木村さんは、自分のことを何と言いましたか？)
   - A. *undou shinkei yokunai shi* (I'm not very athletic, and...)
   - B. *asurechikku ja nai shi* (I'm not 'athletic' (English loanword), and...)
   - C. *undou shinkei hayakunai shi* (my coordination isn't fast, and...)

5. **Yuki runs in from the station every morning. Imagine she asks you, "ashita kara, issho ni eki kara hashiranai?" Answer using tairyoku: say you've got the stamina for it, or that you haven't.** *(say it)*  
   ashita kara, issho ni eki kara hashiranai? (明日から、一緒に駅から走らない？)

### New words

- **undoukai** (運動会): sports day (here, the company's)
- **riree** (リレー): relay race
- **hashiru** (走る): run (hashitteru: is running)
- **mieru** (見える): can see, be visible (mienai: can't see)
- **ouen** (応援): cheering someone on (ouen suru: cheer for)
- **asurechikku** (アスレチック): obstacle-course playground (not 'athletic')

<details>
<summary>Answers and transcript</summary>

1. **B**. Line 3: Tanaka-san asks Mai to run in the relay. Line 7: he says he saw her running fast outside the station this morning, but in line 8 she says she came to work by bus. Lines 10-11: he isn't wearing his glasses and his eyesight is bad (me ga warui), so he got the wrong person. Line 14: Mai realises it was Yuki, and in line 17 he decides to ask Yuki (yuki-san ni tanonde mimasu). A is wrong: Tanaka-san does say the relay doesn't need stamina (line 5), but Mai never agrees. She'll cheer instead (lines 17-18). C is wrong: the runner Tanaka-san saw was Yuki, not Mai. Mai came by bus (line 8). Tanaka-san will ask Yuki (line 17), and Mai will cheer (lines 17-18).
2. Say your eyesight is bad: me ga warui (in casual speech, me warui). You could add that you left your glasses at home, or, if your eyes have got worse lately, say me ga waruku natta. For example: *un, me warui n da. megane, uchi ni wasurechatta.* (Yeah, my eyesight's bad. I left my glasses at home.); *un, saikin me ga waruku natta n da.* (Yeah, my eyes have got worse lately.). In lines 11-12, Tanaka-san and Mai both say 'me ga warui' to explain why they can't see, and Tanaka-san has left his glasses at home (uchi ni wasurete kita), like the first model answer. The eyes are the subject and warui describes them, so don't say 'warui me o motteru' (have bad eyes). In casual speech ga drops (me warui). 'Got worse' is the -ku naru form, me ga waruku natta, not warui ni natta. Mai is talking to you casually as a friend, so a casual answer fits.
3. **C**. Line 5: ashi ga hayai puts the speed in the legs, so it means someone who runs fast. Tanaka-san thinks this because, in line 7, he says he saw her running outside the station and she was really fast (sugoku hayakatta). In line 16 Mai says the same thing about Yuki (ashi mo hayai). A confuses this with the other meaning of hayai, 'early'. B would be shigoto ga hayai (a fast worker), and nobody talks about how fast anyone works.
4. **A**. Line 4: 'undou shinkei yokunai shi'. undou shinkei (literally 'motor nerves') goes with ii, here in the negative yokunai, to mean she's not athletic. Tanaka-san uses the same pair in line 15 (undou shinkei ga yosasou) and Mai uses it in line 16 (undou shinkei mo ii). B is wrong: in Japanese asurechikku means a playground obstacle course, not 'athletic', and Mai doesn't say it. C is wrong: hayai is for speed (ashi ga hayai), while coordination takes ii, not hayai.
5. Use tairyoku with aru or nai, dropping ga in casual speech: tairyoku nai kara... to say no, or tairyoku aru yo to say yes. For example: *e, muri muri. watashi, tairyoku nai kara.* (What? No way. I've got no stamina.); *ii ne! kou miete mo, kekkou tairyoku aru yo.* (Sounds good! I may not look it, but I've got a fair bit of stamina.). Stamina takes aru or nai, not ii. Mai says 'tairyoku mo nai' about herself (line 4) and 'tairyoku mo aru' about Yuki (line 16), and lines 5 and 18 use 'tairyoku ga nakute mo daijoubu' (you don't need stamina). Don't say fitto (in Japanese that's how clothes fit) or genki (well, healthy) for stamina. Yuki asks casually, so answer casually.

|---|---|---|---|

| 3 | Tanaka-san | riree ni deru hito ga, hitori tarinai n desu. kimura-san, dete moraemasen ka? | We're one runner short for the relay. Could you run in it? |
| 4 | Mai | e, watashi desu ka? undou shinkei yokunai shi, tairyoku mo nai n desu yo. | What, me? I'm not very athletic, and I've got no stamina either. |

| 6 | Mai | iya, sonna koto nai desu yo. | Oh no, not really. |

| 15 | Tanaka-san | yuki-san desu ka. sou ieba, undou shinkei ga yosasou desu ne. | Yuki, huh. Come to think of it, she does seem athletic. |
| 16 | Mai | hai. undou shinkei mo ii shi, ashi mo hayai shi, tairyoku mo aru n desu yo. | She is. She's athletic, she's fast, and she's got stamina too. |
| 17 | Tanaka-san | jaa, yuki-san ni tanonde mimasu. kimura-san wa, ouen o onegai shimasu ne. | Then I'll ask Yuki. And Kimura-san, I'm counting on you for the cheering. |
| 18 | Mai | hai! ouen nara, tairyoku ga nakute mo daijoubu desu ne. | Sure! At least cheering doesn't take any stamina. |
